> That is a misrepresentation of what the Dunning-Kruger effect is really about. The Dunning-Kruger effect typically occurs when people first acquire some knowledge in a specific field: their confidence rises dramatically early on, often exceeding their actual mastery of the topic. Later, they enter the “Dunning-Kruger valley,” where their confidence drops sharply—an overcorrection. This effect is not about being stupid or smart; even highly intelligent people experience it. I could argue that the statement about the Dunning-Kruger effect itself demonstrates a Dunning-Kruger peak: expressing confidence in understanding the concept without fully mastering it.

> @rishikeshmuppana1155 Your summation is dead on. I went to school in what was once a farming community that became desirable in Connecticut. The disparity in treatment wasn't even hid. I never sat in detention for something like a scuffle or chewing gum in class with a lawyer or doctors son, never. They were doing the same things, but got a pat on the head. That school is no longer in operation but was used in one of Wes Craven's last horror films.   
> My wife on the other hand, got into a magnet school for geniuses in the D.C. area mostly catering to wealthy kids from America and abroad. They were being taught by professors and were doing things like building working electric cars and being prepped to take over the world. She decided that wasn't for her after graduating. She looked up her former classmates. They are all C.E.O's of Netflix, Microsoft, heads of medicine at John Hopkins, etc,   She got in on a scholarship and wasn't wealthy. She described watching some of these teens from places like Saudi Arabia get away with committing vehicular manslaughter in their sports car while intoxicated, but not be charged because their parents had diplomatic immunity, and then they showed up to school on Monday like nothing happened. THAT is a perfect example of living in two very different worlds.

### **@caballerorosemariejeany.9629** | 👍 64 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgxRo_2t86VJSTrGiTJ4AaABAg)

I'm currently an intern at this company. I'm a student from a public university while my bosses and supervisors came from big, private schools in our country. Some of the things they ask me are, 

"Why do people from your school not apply to any bigger companies or international scholarships?" 
"Students from your school rarely speaks up when in a meeting, is there something wrong?"
"Why are you people so afraid of failing and taking risks?"

For context, the previous batch of interns they had from our university also exhibit the same characteristics that we, the new interns, are showing. We don't speak much, we never ask questions, we' re afraid to apply to bigger companies, etc. My supervisors and bosses mean well. They even recommended us to go and have higher ambitions, pursue graduate studies abroad, and apply to more renowned companies, saying that we can put their names and contacts as our reference number.

After hearing their advice, I went on to study how to communicate better. But so far, none of the books or yt vids worked. Not until I came across this lecture. And I finally had the answers to all my bosses' questions. 25 mins in and I'm getting emotional over a fctking lecture about parenting styles. HAHAHHAHAA This is such an eye-opener to me. Hats-off to your Professor!!! I finally understand why we are the way we are. I finally understand that                                                                                                                    I'm not really the faulty robot that I thought I was, meant to be thrown away for being useless and timid.

> ​@TarlWar-sf8yr and on the other side of that coin, if the kid hadn't eaten in a day and takes the marshmallow, the experiment assumes that they have bad impulse control, when in reality hes just hungry and made a measured decision to take the marshmallow. Or maybe its just an arrogant kid who thinks they can talk the researcher into giving them another one or knows their parents will bjy them more marshmallows afterwards, so 1 vs 2 doesnt really matter to them in the moment. These are calculated acts rather than an impulsive ones. Maybe some of them just weren't listening when they were told they'll get another one, bc thats something kids tend to do a lot. There are plenty of ways that "taking the marshmallow" can be a result of something besides simple impulse control  
>   
> Taking the marshmallow doesnt inherently signal "poor impulse control" multiple factors can play in, and it can be a conscious decision rather than an impulsive act. Thats what youre missing here when you pretend its purely genetic  
>   
> A good control measure would involve feeding the kids similar meals before offering the marshmallow or something along those lines but without something like that in place, these are pretty valid critiques

### **@eugenemorrison2720** | 👍 108 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgwNWFDZPP7QTGGFx1h4AaABAg)

I grew up very poor in the US. A family of 4 in the 1980s and 90s on one income from my dad who at most grossed about $14K per year when he left his job in the early 90s. As a 5 year old I knew which truck from the electric company was coming to turn off our power because we couldn't pay the bill, among all the other things we couldn't pay for. We lived in constant volatility and stress and anxiety, which led to constant fights and yelling inside the house. It never felt safe or secure. And I feel the effects of all those things today in my 40s, as someone who earns almost 10x (in my househould between my partner and I, no children) what my parents did, and with a partner who is well-adjusted and grew up in a middleclass household. It's hard to escape the anxiety that lifestyle built into me as a kid. Not to mention the emotional abuse of being made fun of by many of the kids in school because my clothes and shoes were cheap from a garage sale or the salvation army.

And I did what Jiang did: Abandon my community to get educated in an environment where I felt I could find success. And while I wouldn't call myself successful by American standards, I am much more successful than if I had stayed in that small town of 500 people who all still earn about $25K per year on average.

>   
> In Rich Dad Poor Dad, Kiyosaki separates debt into “good” debt (positive cash-flow assets ex real estate) and “bad” debt (consumption: ex car). The issue with this isn’t inherently good or bad; it depends on access, cost, and risk.  
>   
> Wealthy people can borrow large sums at low rates and use strategies like buy-borrow-die to build wealth. What Kiyosaki fails to mention is how high-risk this is for most people. For the average borrower, debt is expensive and dangerous. In Canada consumer interest rates frequently surpasses 20%.  
>   
> Strategies like buy-borrow-die require accurately projecting cash flow, inflation, and interest at the same time. Inflation is uncertain, cash-flow projections are only estimates. A rich person can absorb losses but the average person risks their livelihood to do the same.

### **@christopheryu4148** | 👍 32 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgzBb7WlarTAZoDdY754AaABAg)

Summarize this video: 
Professor Jiang's lecture on "Game Theory #3: Rich Dad, Poor Dad" explores the question of who succeeds and why (0:00). He discusses various theories of success, the impact of socioeconomic status on development, and the underlying mechanisms of social stability and change.

Here's a breakdown of the key points:

Traditional Theories of Success

Delayed Gratification/Self-Control: Based on Walter Mischel's Marshmallow Test (0:31), this theory suggests that individuals who can delay immediate rewards for greater long-term gains tend to be more successful in various aspects of life, including academics, careers, and relationships (1:51).
Growth Mindset/Resilience: Carol Dweck's Mindset theory (4:00) posits that people with a "growth mindset" view failures as opportunities for learning and improvement, leading to greater persistence and eventual success, unlike those with a "fixed mindset" who give up easily (4:11).
Deliberate Practice/Self-Assessment: K. Anders Ericsson's work on "deliberate practice" emphasizes strategic effort, goal setting, and continuous self-reflection to identify weaknesses and improve performance (5:21). The Dunning-Kruger effect (7:21) is mentioned to illustrate the difficulty of accurate self-assessment, especially for those lacking competence.
Correlation vs. Causation in Success (9:49)
Professor Jiang argues that while successful people often exhibit traits like self-control, resilience, and deliberate practice, these traits are often effects of success, not necessarily causes. He emphasizes that correlation does not equal causation (9:57). For example, getting up early doesn't cause success; successful people often get up early because they are motivated (10:12).

Socioeconomic Status and Success (11:23)
The lecture highlights that rich people are much more likely to succeed than poor people, regardless of academic performance (11:31). This is attributed to fundamental differences in parenting strategies:

Communication: Rich parents tend to speak to their children more, using a higher vocabulary and longer sentences (12:11), fostering better communication skills. Poor parents often use more direct commands (12:40).
Attitude: Rich parents adopt a friendly and explanatory approach (12:49), making children feel respected and safe, which translates into positive interactions with authority figures like teachers (14:16). Poor parents often use an authoritarian or commanding attitude, which can create stress and fear in children (13:46).
Stability vs. Volatility: Rich parents, due to financial resources, can offer stability and keep promises (15:00), building trust. Poor parents often face financial constraints, leading to volatility and an inability to consistently keep promises (15:29).
Reinterpreting Success Traits through a Socioeconomic Lens (15:44)
Professor Jiang re-evaluates the traditional theories of success based on these parenting differences:

Marshmallow Test (Self-Control): It's reframed as a test of trust in authority figures rather than just self-control (16:05). Rich kids, experiencing more stability, trust that a second marshmallow will be provided, while poor kids, accustomed to broken promises, rationally choose immediate gratification (16:27).
Resilience: The belief that the world will help you is crucial for resilience (17:12). Rich kids, with supportive environments, can afford to be resilient, knowing help is available if they fail. Poor kids often learn that failure means no one will help them (17:28).
Self-Assessment: Stress significantly hinders self-reflection (17:53). Poor children living under constant stress find it hard to look inward without focusing on pain and anxiety.
Social Hierarchy and Its Implications (19:35)
Society is presented as a hierarchy divided between the rich and the poor, with distinct "games" being played (19:38).

Poor: Learn to obey authority to survive (20:06). Their parenting strategies are geared towards ensuring their children fit into a system where compliance is key (21:00), dealing with figures like police, bosses, and family (23:03).
Rich: Learn to negotiate with authority to maximize outcomes (20:16). Their parenting fosters debate, argument, and respect for their child's agency (21:15).
Social Mobility and Revolution (30:28)
Professor Jiang addresses the question of social mobility for the poor. While individual success stories exist, they often require abandoning one's community (29:33) or taking high risks like war or revolution (30:28). The best traditional way of social mobility is marrying up (31:03). He emphasizes that individual success stories are often due to luck (32:16) and are the exception, not the rule (32:57).

He then explains why societies collapse despite appearing stable:

Elite Overproduction: The rich, constantly seeking maximum outcomes, eventually lead to a situation where there are too many powerful individuals vying for too few top positions (37:15).
Catalysts for Revolution: Revolutions are typically led by the "have some" (middle class or aspirational elite) who align with the "have-nots" (poor) against the "have a lot" (established elite) (38:05). Common problems that fuel revolution include indebtedness, slavery, and landlessness (40:32). Revolution promises to clear debts, provide land, and end slavery (42:00). Historical examples like the Chinese Revolution, the Islamic Revolution, and Julius Caesar's popularity are cited (42:21). Donald Trump's popularity is also framed in this context (43:48).
Game Reset (50:33)
Social mobility is crucial for societal stability and prosperity (46:18). However, those in power tend to rig the system to benefit their children, limiting opportunities for others (47:50). When social mobility stagnates, and people cannot climb the ladder, they resort to revolution, which serves as a "game reset" (50:33), allowing for a new cycle of social mobility before the system is rigged again (51:18). This cycle of rigging the game and then resetting it through revolution is presented as the course of human history (51:18).

### **@high-reshallucinations**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgyxctbXZbTTg2mqXrJ4AaABAg)

Across his videos (geopolitics, success, society), his tone is very consistent:

🎭 A. “Revealing hidden truth”

“what they don’t tell you…”

“this is how the world actually works”

“everything you’ve learned is wrong”

👉 This creates:

intellectual authority + exclusivity

⚫ B. Apocalyptic / high-stakes framing

“end of the world”

“collapse”

“war for humanity”

“system is broken”

👉 This amplifies:

urgency

emotional engagement

🧑‍🏫 C. Teacher persona (very effective)

structured explanations

simple frameworks

confident delivery

no hesitation

👉 This builds:

trust + clarity = perceived intelligence

🎯 D. Absolute confidence

He rarely says:

“maybe”

“uncertain”

“one possibility”

Instead:

“this will happen”

“this is how it works”

👉 That’s persuasive—but risky.

🧠 2. His quality of thought

This is where you need nuance.

✅ Where he’s genuinely strong
1. Pattern recognition

He’s good at:

spotting recurring structures

linking micro (family) → macro (society)

simplifying complex systems

Example:

parenting → behavior → class reproduction

👉 That’s legitimately insightful.

2. Reframing common ideas

He flips familiar concepts:

success traits → result, not cause

war → strategic trap

hierarchy → self-reinforcing system

👉 This is why he feels “mind-opening”

3. Narrative construction

He builds:

clean models

clear cause-effect chains

memorable analogies

👉 This is elite-level communication

❌ Where his thinking weakens
1. Over-compression of reality

He reduces:

complex systems → 1–2 variables

Examples:

success → class structure

war → single strategic trap

history → repeating pattern

👉 This creates:

clarity at the cost of accuracy

2. Lack of empirical grounding

He:

references studies loosely

rarely shows data

doesn’t engage counter-evidence

👉 It sounds academic but isn’t rigorous.

3. Deterministic bias

Everything becomes:

inevitable

structural

locked-in

👉 This removes:

randomness

policy variation

human unpredictability

4. Narrative > truth priority

His models are:

elegant

coherent

satisfying

But reality is:

inconsistent

messy

contradictory

👉 He favors:

a good story over a messy truth

⚠ 3. His recurring contradictions (across videos)

These show up again and again:

🔁 A. Determinism vs agency

He says:

“individuals don’t matter”

“system determines everything”

But also:

“you must strategize”

“you can escape”

“leaders shape outcomes”

👉 He switches depending on the point.

🔁 B. Inevitability vs prediction confidence

He claims:

outcomes are inevitable

But also:

makes very specific predictions

with high confidence

👉 If systems are that complex, certainty should be lower.

🔁 C. Complexity vs simplification

He says:

“the world is complex”

Then explains it with:

2–3 variables

fixed roles

clean outcomes

👉 That tension is constant.

🔁 D. Structure vs intention

Sometimes:

systems emerge naturally

Other times:

elites are actively controlling outcomes

👉 These are different theories—but he blends them.

🔁 E. Zero-sum vs growth

He often assumes:

hierarchy = zero-sum

But also:

economies grow

opportunities expand

👉 This is a foundational inconsistency.

🔁 F. Anti-self-help vs partial self-help

He rejects:

hard work

mindset

discipline

But still says:

be strategic

take risks

position yourself

👉 He critiques self-help… while partially reproducing it.

🧠 4. Why he feels so convincing

This is the key insight.

He combines:

✔ Truth fragments

inequality matters

structure matters

incentives shape behavior

✔ With strong delivery

confident

clear

structured

✔ And narrative closure

everything fits

no loose ends

no uncertainty

👉 Your brain interprets that as:

“this must be deeply true”

🎯 5. What he actually is (best description)

He is not:

a rigorous academic analyst

a data-driven researcher

He is closer to:

🧠 a high-level systems storyteller

Specifically:

uses real concepts

builds simplified models

pushes them to extremes

⚖ Final verdict
👍 Strengths:

excellent communicator

strong pattern recognition

thought-provoking frameworks

challenges naive beliefs

👎 Weaknesses:

overconfident

oversimplified

internally inconsistent

weak on evidence

leans deterministic

🧭 Bottom line

He’s not “BS”… but he’s also not “truth.”

He’s:

a compelling interpreter of reality who sacrifices precision for clarity and impact

That rich and poor part is HUGELY reductive. I like this guy, but there are loads of other factors. And I have seen rich families where the kid is shouted at, scorned and not shown friendliness

Also, the marshmallow test isn’t just about trust, it’s about impulse control and instant gratification. 

Just a good reminder that just because you respect someone’s authority, like I do this professor, doesn’t mean he’s necessarily right in everything he says

There is a lot I agree with too btw

> **@M0hh_** | 👍 2 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=Ugyh9AJjEqDPvG2qv_x4AaABAg.AU7NgoecxkRAUEjj4V6H7F)  
>
> I like this comment a lot. Criticism is a dead art nowadays.  
> I think he was just adding in factors that could affect the outcome of the marshmallow test. So he spoke about the idea of instant gratification but he also mentioned another factor that could alter the behavior of the child in that situation, and it's absolutely crucial to understand, because a poor child gets programmed to take advantage of the presented opportunity at the moment because it may no longer be there later, so that lack of stability needs to be mentioned, it's not that it's the "answer", it's that it shapes the answer alongside the other factors such as instant gratification.  
>   
> And for the rich families' mannerism, i think it was an overgeneralization but it's kind of accurate, the less stress the parent is in, the more comfort they have, the more they have the privilege of actually taking on the toll of their children's mistakes, and so they allow their children to make mistakes and guide them, rather than impulsively being shook/angry when the child makes a mistake, the poor parent rarely has the emotional/physical capacity to take on any more problems.  
> But I'm overgeneralizing as well here; it's just something to think about rather than a rule to shape a worldview on. The way the parent was raised, how educated, self-aware, and mentally stable they are, are much more important factors thant their economic situation, we're just taking into account that economic status affects those things hugely, some thinkers would go as far as to say that it is the most important factor, but from a more central perspective, we can atleast acknowledge that they do have significance. And I think that's something prof. Jiang realizes, but when you're explaining an idea in one lecture, it's hard to keep adding this much nuance to every point you make, you may need to leave that up to the listener.

### **@SokHainRich009**

You are an amazing professor, my dad, who went to the University of France in the 1970s, did not raise me like in a rich family.  He raised me exactly how you taught in your class, very stern, strict, subjugating his kid into fear with insult, beating, and he always said that if I don't beat you when you do wrong, you will end up in jail when you grow up.  So his ways of disciplining his children is 1000% like what you taught in class.  The diferent mind set of the Poor parents vs the Rich parents raising their kids is night and day.  I really like the rich parenting, I wish I were born in a rich family.  I loathe being raised by my dad in a poor family.   Even to this day, I shudder just to think what I went through when I was young, raised by my poor dad.  It is a nightmare.  I remembered going to school at an early age. I heard my dad telling the teacher that if I misbehave, the teachers have the right to discipline me as he or she wishes.  I fear going to school.  And I was so afraid of doing wrong or not abiding by the school rules.  Afraid that I will get the beating from the teachers and my dad at the same time.  And when I turned 30, I wanted so much to be rich, I read so many Rich Dad and Poor Dad books, so from my experience in opening my 1st business and failing, Rich Dad and Poor Dad can only be done when I have lots of money, so that when I fail in one business, I have more money to open a second business, a third, and a fourth, and a fifth, until I finally reach success.   Unfortunately, I only have money from my savings to start a one-time business, so if I fail in my 1st business, then I don't have any more money to start my 2nd business, nor my 3rd nor my 4th nor my 5th.  Rich Dad and Poor Dad, in his book, wrote that it takes an entrepreneur 5 business failures to learn from his own mistakes and finally become successful in his 5th business.  Unfortunately, I don't have that kind of money to keep opening after I fail with my 1st business.  In the end, I am still in the rat race and still struggling to reach my financial freedom.

### **@adventures_with_Aussie_Aaron** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=Ugz2XIzgsFTSgPsq2wp4AaABAg)

As someone who grew up poor—constantly moving because of evictions, faced multiple bouts of homelessness, and dealt with food insecurity—I've seen how real those stereotypes can feel on average. That said, not having money doesn't have to be an insurmountable barrier to success. If you have the foresight, resilience, and mindset to keep improving yourself, you can still rise. If your definition of success is financial independence or true freedom, figure out how to create real economic value in the world—and get paid for it. What skills do you have or you can develop which can be monetised? Starting with extra hurdles means you simply can't afford to make the same mistakes others do. Learn those hard lessons by observing them instead of living them, or you'll get left behind. Ultimately, if you're willing to do what others aren't prepared to do, things will start going your way.

For the record I would not have eaten the marshmallow. I would have collected as many as I could and then tried to sell it to the person with the highest willingness to pay.

I agree, in part, with what Professor Jiang presents: Islam is, in essence, a profound revolution. It seeks to liberate humanity from everything that diminishes its true status by calling it to submit to God alone—freeing it from submission to other humans, power, desire, or greed, whatever form that “other” may take. However, this vision is far deeper and more comprehensive than the simplified way it was presented.


This leads to the central question: was the Prophet Muhammad (peace be upon him) truly a prophet of God, and is the Quran truly a divine revelation? I encourage everyone to read the Quran for themselves and reach their own conclusion.


What you may find is a text rich in deep reasoning, filled with sophisticated and mind-blowing, mind -challenging arguments, and containing striking reflections that continue to invite contemplation. It also includes references that many associate with scientific discoveries identified much later ( many were discovered only in the late 20th century  —such as the expansion of the universe and the stages of embryonic development—which, strongly point to its divine origin.


Furthermore, Islam is not an isolated message; rather, it is the continuation of the same call carried by earlier prophets—Adam, Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus (peace be upon them all). It carries forward the same core message: the oneness of God and the dignity of mankind through sincere devotion to Him alone.

### **@Blueprint30App**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgzZUeqyRLuFfLNq3aF4AaABAg)

Theories of Success (and why they fail to explain inequality)

* Marshmallow Test (Walter Mischel): Success linked to delayed gratification and self-control.
* Mindset (Carol Dweck): Success linked to a "growth mindset" (resilience) vs. "fixed mindset."
* Deliberate Practice (Anders Ericsson): Success linked to strategic practice and self-reflection.
* Dunning-Kruger Effect: Incompetent people lack the self-awareness to know they are incompetent.
* Failure of Application: Teaching these skills (self-control, resilience) to poor students does not statistically improve their long-term success.
* Correlation vs. Causation: Successful people have these traits because they are already successful/rich, not necessarily the other way around.

Rich vs. Poor Parenting Strategies

* Vocabulary: Rich parents use complex language/reasoning; poor parents use commands/short directives.
* Attitude: Rich parents are friendly/negotiating; poor parents are authoritarian/demanding.
* Stability: Rich parents offer stability (keep promises); poor parents offer volatility (cannot keep promises due to financial stress).
* Result: Rich kids learn to negotiate and trust the world. Poor kids learn to obey authority and distrust the future.
* Rational Adaptations:
* Poor kids eating the marshmallow immediately is a rational response to an unstable environment where promises are rarely kept.
* Poor parents use authoritarian styles to protect children from police/bosses who require total obedience for survival.


The Game of Society

* Hierarchy: Society is a game where the rich negotiate for maximum outcome, and the poor obey for survival (minimum outcome).
* Social Mobility: The most effective stabilizer for society. If talent can rise, society remains stable and prosperous.
* Elite Overproduction:
* Rich families rig the system to ensure their children inherit power, blocking social mobility for talented poor people.
* When the top is full and mobility stops, the "game" breaks.

* Revolution:
* Occurs when the "have-nots" (often led by a frustrated segment of the elite/middle class) reset the game.
* Drivers of Revolution: Indebtedness, landlessness, and slavery (loss of autonomy).
* Goal of Revolution: Debt cancellation, land redistribution, and freedom.


Pathways for the Poor

* Leaving Community: Immigrating to a place with high mobility (high risk, requires extreme individualism).
* Military/War: High-risk, high-reward path to status.
* Marrying Up: Traditionally a primary method of social mobility.
* Luck: Success for the poor often relies on being in the right place to get lucky, rather than just hard work.

As someone who grew up poor but went to private school, I 100% agree with his analysis, in private school we are made to think that we have the world at our fingertips, that we can be anyone we want to be, where else in public school we are taught that the nail that sticks out get hammered. In public schools, punishment are dished out often (including corporal punishment) to keep students in line or to make them do something, but that was not the case in private schools, here we were motivated to do so, also another thing i noticed is that lot of people who grew up in rich family have npc syndrome, where they think they are gifts to the world, even if they are mediocre (but they often have some high achievement but often told their achievement is lot more spectacular than it is). >
> I was raised with a hard-working and highly suvcessful abusive father and a laid back loving mother from a wealthier family. For ideological and practical reasons, they chose public school for me, which is not bad but not excellent in my country. The teachers could represent both the authoritarian or negociating style for me, and I grew up with a dislike for male authority figures and a sense of entitlement. I'm also deaf in one ear, female and racialized, one heck of an intersectionality example. Now, I have a postgraduate degree, but I'm poorer than my parents, and generally unfulfilled.   
>   
> This lecture is adequately simplified for his students and the timeframe. But he did fail to point out several other factors that weren’t taken into account. There are micro factors like being disabled and racialized and a victim of bullying and macro factors like inflation, ressource crisis, international conflicts. The problem with international migration which for him seemed a factor for success, is for example that your children can become racialized. He also didn’t address the issue of patriarchy and whether or not his wife works. The freedom he allows his children was also the ́’́freedom’ of some poor people I know who grew up going back to an empty home after school because the parents were working and who could play freely or meet dangerous people who took advantage of the the parents’ absence. If his wife doesn’t work, does it mean that successful parenting is the woman at home? etc, etc.

### **@YPEhms**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgxlD1h2rowDxMeimc14AaABAg)

Highlights
0:28 Teste do marshmallow e gratificação adiada

Crianças que resistem à tentação tendem a ter melhores resultados acadêmicos e profissionais.

O experimento sugere que autocontrole e planejamento de longo prazo são vistos como chaves para o sucesso.

4:02 Mentalidade de crescimento vs. mentalidade fixa

Carol Dweck mostra que pessoas com mentalidade de crescimento aprendem com erros e persistem.

Já as de mentalidade fixa desistem diante do fracasso.

5:29 Prática deliberada e autoavaliação

Anders Ericsson defende que sucesso vem de prática estratégica e reflexão constante.

O efeito Dunning-Kruger mostra que muitos não conseguem avaliar corretamente suas próprias habilidades.

12:15 Diferenças na criação entre ricos e pobres

Pais ricos oferecem vocabulário sofisticado, atitudes amigáveis e estabilidade.

Pais pobres tendem a ser mais autoritários e instáveis, o que afeta a confiança das crianças.

16:07 Reinterpretação do teste do marshmallow

Não mede autocontrole, mas confiança em figuras de autoridade.

Crianças pobres comem o doce porque sabem que promessas muitas vezes não são cumpridas.

29:01 Mobilidade social e exceções

Alguns pobres conseguem ascender por sorte, guerra, casamento ou imigração.

Porém, isso é raro e arriscado; a maioria permanece na mesma classe social.

36:07 Revoluções e colapso social

Revoluções surgem quando elites em excesso competem pelo poder.

Promessas de cancelar dívidas, redistribuir terras e acabar com a escravidão são recorrentes na história.

46:18 Mobilidade social como chave da estabilidade

Sociedades prosperam quando permitem ascensão baseada em mérito.

Mas elites tendem a fechar o sistema para garantir poder aos próprios filhos, levando a revoluções.

### **@nishanacht** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=Ugx8JkNcg8oAObQC5wh4AaABAg)

what's really funny is that when prof j is talking about parenting, Im just tearing up thinking about my upbringing in the USbc its the same in Indian families. Parents are from India, Dad was the independent risk-taker who wanted to come here and was very bright and worked his arse off and he did very well over decades of work but he came over with $$7 in his pocket for real. Worked 2 jobs while in grad school here. His wife, my Mother never wanted to leave India, very family and community minded. but also worked 12 hours a day at a daycare but also raised her kids and did all the home stuff.   For daughters, this  can be a huge conflict bc Father demands everything of us regardless of male or female children. Mother did too, but leaving the family for better career opportunities or to travel, she never ever saw the value. 

I don't know what im really saying but I realized during this lecture that its funny bc many immigrants come to the US, many independent people, but then they bring over an entire generation of their families and then only hang out with their own. So they all move here... just to conform again within their own communities.

### **@fromviennawithlove-g5p**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgxcTV2dfGbLo3d1txx4AaABAg)

Very interesting. I like to add the following.

When we examine revolutions within the British Empire, we see that they began during periods of famine. The Irish Potato Famine and the famines in India, for example, created widespread revolts. As long as people maintain a minimum level of subsistence, they seem unwilling to risk their lives to overthrow a government. Revolutions tend to arise when individuals are forced to choose between certain death from hunger and the smaller, uncertain risk of dying in revolt.

It is important to note that in both Ireland and India, only certain regions experienced severe hunger. The British authorities chose not to redistribute surplus harvests from unaffected areas, preferring instead to export them to preserve annual profits. As a result, populations that had once feared their rulers began to hate them.

As Niccolò Machiavelli argues in The Prince, fear can be an effective tool for maintaining power. However, he also warns that when fear turns into hatred, a ruler’s downfall becomes likely.

The trouble here is you're emphasizing on the success cases when it comes to children who came from wealth however, there is an absolute mountain of examples supporting the contrary.

Many failures came from wealthy parents, either because their parents were too busy focusing on their career, business, themselves etc

Overly laid back parenting, not instilling discipline or desperately trying to shelter their kids from the pain and struggle they endured. I would go out on a limb, while being quite generous, and state it's 50/50 in terms of success due to wealthy parents. 

Maybe they'll have some success later on via funding from parents but inevitably they'll stumble due to the lack of crucial, difficult experiences that are required to shape them into a resilient person - my example being the countless successful businesses that were run into the ground by a persons children.

Impressive lecture, most of what was said makes sense and really gets one to think about how societal groups operate, and how human behavior shapes the systems that we're apart of.
But I have one issue with the comment at 42:38 about how the religion of Islam is just a revolution to "reset the game," that's not consistent with what actually happened at the beginning of the religion, in fact the followers of the prophet Mohammad (PBUH,) early on, lost their homes, their access to food and social status for 10 years in Mecca and for a long time after that in Medinah. The Prophet himself was of a high status family and had considerable wealth, but he lost all of it during prosecution in Mecca, and in Medinah he donated almost everything he had to those in need. In Mecca he was offered higher status positions to stop calling people to Islam but he refused. 
From those three points, in Islam, Interest is prohibited of the wisdom that, as mentioned in the lecture, it corrupts and inhibits society by keeping the poor forever chained to the rich's control and gaining a reward with no effort/risk.

Hello! First, thank you so much for sharing these lectures fo us. I finished Secret History in four days, and I'm now on Game Theory. I've learned more from these lectures than I learned in my formal education and it has sincerely changed my life and my understanding of not only today, but how and why we got here, and how it will likely end (although every day is quite the surprise in reality).

With that said, I'm hoping you can explain to me why you use the term Dissociative Personality Disorder instead of Psycopathy, or Borderline Personality, or Narcissistic Personality Disorder? I ask this because A LOT of us dissociate as a result of childhood trauma, but that does not mean DID/DPD or two or more distinct personalities. My concern is that people who don't know this will come to believe that those people who dissociate, (because historically it has saved their lives, and occasional dissociation is much more common),  have  this VERY specific and rare disorder, which is simply not true. I also very seriously doubt most of our politicians have DID but perhaps there is some insight to this I'm not aware of.

Also, since I'm here, we no longer use terms like prostitution, rather sex work is a more accurate term. Women who have no choice in the matter due to drug addiction etc are often referred to as survival sex workers. Women who are trapped are sex slaves. 

My apologies if you have already addressed these issues, if so, pointing me to a previous response would be greatly appreciated!

Thank you so much for your brilliant work!

The video was insightful, but it left me with a question.
 If we define success only in terms of being rich or poor, aren’t we unconsciously trapping ourselves in a life that is always chasing future rewards?

A successful man in a society may be a failure as a being.

So what should one choose?
Should we focus on thinking strategically to win in this system,
or should we wake up to the possibility that there is nothing to win at all—
and instead care only about living a life where we are truly happy?
I may be wrong, but this is the dilemma I’m trying to understand.

"Marshmallow test is not a test of self-control but of the belief in authority to keep its promises."

Very succinct and well-presented. 
It seems that the way Trump is approaching the USA, he is setting conditions for people to lose trust/belief in government/authority to keep its promises (as basic as the Constitution, and the Rule of Law generally).  It remains to be seen which demographics will have their belief in their available authority figures eroded away.  The media plays a significant role for most people, as the transaction between follower and leader is based not on concrete results but rather how the individuals watching the news feel while watching.  Negative, critical news cycles, even if they are taking apart obviously bad actions, will begin to make the viewers despondent because things are not changing, nor do they feel they have a role to play within the events. Instead, trust and belief are eroded. 

What the end result could be is that people who are prone to emotional disregulation become more disregulated, while those with emotional aptitude will get further ahead, simply by doing what they know they ought to do.  This cohort will not feel special for this success, only a bit tired from the discipline.  So, when disregulated people make valid critiques of disparity, and how that disparity is becoming too large, the successful people don't believe, inherently, while the disregulated people believe they've nailed the assessment, both because of the Dunning Kruger effect. 

I have no idea how valid this is.  No references, just inference.

I never did believe that the results of the marshmallow experiment were  purely about self discipline and the ability to 'delay gratification'.   I also thought it probably had a lot to do with other factors like the physiology of the child, and how stable their blood sugar levels were.  Not all people enjoy eating sweet foods.  One of my sons never has liked them, the other cannot get enough.  Likewise with my brother and I.  That being said, the results suggest the children who did resist did do better in life  Perhaps a lot of that was due to having more stable blood sugar, higher dopamine levels, even more sustained energy levels, less inflammation, better sleep etc. Rather than just 'better self discipline'.  And then obviously success or failure compounds itself, with a self reinforcing loop of positive or negative reinforcement.

### **@TounToto**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=Ugxd04bbbt5gxhiEE_J4AaABAg)

I don’t believe education is irrelevant. It clearly has an impact.

But I think its impact is often overstated compared to material and structural advantages.

The decisive difference between children from wealthy and non-wealthy families is not primarily psychological — it is material and institutional. Wealth provides financial capital, access to credit, preferential banking relationships, professional advisors, and powerful social networks. These factors reduce risk, create opportunities, and absorb failure in ways that mindset alone simply cannot replicate.

Education and parenting style may shape confidence, language, or discipline. But those traits operate within a structure. They amplify an existing advantage; they do not replace it.

In other words, education helps convert opportunity into results — but it does not create equal opportunity in the first place.

When discussions focus too heavily on how wealthy parents “speak differently” to their children, it risks shifting attention away from structural realities such as inherited capital, access to institutions, and network effects. That framing can make inequality appear psychological rather than systemic.

So my position is not that education does not matter.
It matters — but mainly as an amplifier of material advantage, not as the primary cause of success.

### **@azinhosseini5172**

I don’t think that’s entirely accurate. I grew up in what you might call a “rich” family in a material sense, but the way my parents treated me was very similar to the patterns you’re describing in poorer households. 

To me, it’s not really about money. It has much more to do with people’s own upbringing, their unresolved trauma, and the emotional environment they were raised in. In my case, my parents came from very difficult, low-resource backgrounds themselves—so in that sense, there might be a connection, but it’s not as simple as wealth = better parenting.

With all due respect, I really value your work, but I don’t agree with this point.

### **@laodeize**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgyPt2TvbhAbTCLIFr94AaABAg)

Berikut adalah penjelasan yang lebih mendalam mengenai poin-poin utama dari video tersebut:
​1. Kritik terhadap Teori Psikologi Tradisional
​Penceramah menjelaskan bahwa sekolah-sekolah sering mencoba mengajarkan perilaku orang sukses kepada siswa yang kurang mampu, namun cara ini sering gagal karena mengabaikan realitas hidup mereka:
​Korelasi vs Kausalitas: Memiliki growth mindset atau bangun jam 4 pagi adalah perilaku orang sukses, tapi melakukan hal itu saja tidak menjamin kekayaan. Sebaliknya, orang yang sudah sukses/kaya lebih mudah memiliki growth mindset karena mereka memiliki "bantalan" jika gagal.
​Efek Dunning-Kruger: Dalam hal penilaian diri, ditemukan bahwa orang yang memiliki kemampuan rendah sering kali merasa dirinya rata-rata atau hebat karena mereka kekurangan kapasitas untuk menyadari kekurangan mereka sendiri.
​2. Perbedaan Pola Asuh Berdasarkan Kelas Sosial
​Video ini menyoroti bagaimana perbedaan kelas menciptakan cara pandang yang berbeda terhadap dunia:
​Negosiasi vs Kepatuhan: Anak-anak kaya diajarkan untuk berdebat dan bernegosiasi dengan orang tua. Hal ini membentuk mentalitas bahwa mereka bisa membentuk dunia sesuai keinginan mereka. Sebaliknya, anak-anak miskin sering diajarkan untuk patuh tanpa bertanya agar tidak mendapat masalah dengan otoritas (seperti polisi atau bos), yang justru menghambat kreativitas dan keberanian mereka di masa depan.
​Stabilitas Janji: Orang tua kaya mampu menepati janji (misalnya liburan) karena mereka punya uang. Orang tua miskin sering terpaksa membatalkan janji karena masalah finansial yang mendadak. Akibatnya, anak miskin belajar bahwa "menunggu" itu berisiko, sehingga mereka lebih memilih keuntungan kecil sekarang daripada keuntungan besar nanti (rasionalitas di balik kegagalan tes marshmallow).
​3. Teori Permainan dalam Struktur Masyarakat
​Penceramah menggunakan Game Theory untuk menjelaskan mengapa masyarakat tetap seperti sekarang:
​Hierarki sebagai Keseimbangan (Equilibrium): Masyarakat cenderung stabil karena setiap kelas belajar cara bertahan hidup di dunianya masing-masing. Namun, sistem ini mulai retak ketika terjadi "Produksi Elit Berlebihan" (Elite Overproduction).
​Penyebab Revolusi: Ketika posisi kekuasaan di puncak sudah penuh, namun jumlah orang yang merasa "layak" menjadi elit terus bertambah, maka kelompok elit yang tersisih ini akan memimpin rakyat (yang sedang menderita karena hutang dan kemiskinan) untuk menghancurkan sistem lama.
​Hutang dan Tanah: Secara historis, tiga faktor utama yang memicu kemarahan rakyat adalah beban hutang yang tidak mungkin terbayar, kehilangan kepemilikan tanah, dan perhambaan. Tokoh seperti Julius Caesar atau bahkan Donald Trump disebut sebagai contoh pemimpin yang menggunakan narasi "menghapus beban rakyat" untuk mendapatkan kekuasaan.
​4. Mobiliti Sosial dan "Game Reset"
​Ketidakmungkinan Mobiliti: Seiring berjalannya waktu, sebuah dinasti atau sistem akan menjadi korup karena orang-orang di puncak ingin anak-anak mereka mewarisi posisi tersebut, meskipun anak-anak tersebut tidak berbakat. Mereka akan "mencurangi" sistem (seperti ujian masuk atau akses pekerjaan) sehingga orang berbakat dari kelas bawah tidak bisa naik.
​Revolusi sebagai Reset: Ketika jalur damai untuk naik kelas sudah tertutup total, maka jalan satu-satunya bagi orang-orang ambisius dari kelas bawah adalah melakukan revolusi atau "Game Reset" untuk memulai permainan baru di mana mobilitas sosial kembali terbuka.
​Secara keseluruhan, pesan video ini cukup provokatif: bahwa keberuntungan (luck) dan struktur sosial berperan jauh lebih besar dalam kesuksesan dibandingkan sekadar kerja keras atau karakter individu saja.

### **@DanzeMusic**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgwRnveJNTzL-w8EKs54AaABAg)

I love Prof Jiang’s videos but this one I disagree with completely. For one, rich parents aren’t always nice to their children, and poor parents aren’t always bad to their kids. Most would considered my parents to be rich but I was treated like your description of how poor parents treat their children since the day I was born. In fact, from observing how some poor parents treated their kids from school, I was quite jealous of them. Somehow my other 3 brothers were treated very well unlike me, and my second brother did really well at school constantly and ended up getting into Cambridge medical school without even needed to make any effort. My younger didn’t do so well at school when he was younger but ended up graduated with a 1st degree hon from imperial college London literally by studying damn hard. After graduating from medical school, my elder brother became depressed and didn’t really care about his career at all. My younger brother seemed very ambitious and end up being very successful as a banker and the department head of an international bank. After my parents passed away, my elder died from cancer very soon after at the age of 55, and 4 months later, my younger brother took his own life. Now I realized my younger brother wasn’t really very ambitious, but he just believed that he would be happy if he was. But even with all the achievements  they had in life, they were both quite unhappy. As for me, I think I could have been quite successful if I  really wanted to be, but I just don’t get any joy from success or achievement, wealth and power are quite meaningless for me. However, at the age of 63, I  think I am quite happy with my life, even with my very unhappy childhood. So I guess for some people, success is quite meaningless, and definitely not to be desired, even for rich kids. 🤔

### **@RaghadAlAwashiz**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgzizLgciQXCBxBK4T94AaABAg)

42:59

I find it frustrating that your argument reflects a superficial reading and applies an external framework without sufficient historical grounding. The proposed connection between class based revolutionary theory and the case of Prophet Muhammad peace be upon him does not appear to hold under scrutiny.

First, he explicitly rejected offers of wealth, power, and status from the Quraysh, which directly contradicts any claim of material motivation. Second, rather than gaining advantage, he endured economic boycott, persecution, and personal loss. Third, his message challenged the existing social and economic order rather than exploiting it.

Fourth, his teachings were not framed in a way that appealed to immediate material benefit or popular desire, but instead often demanded difficult moral and social change. Fifth, his early followers came from varied social and economic backgrounds, which does not support the idea of a unified class movement. Sixth, there is no clear pattern of redistributing wealth in favor of a specific class in the manner suggested by such theories.

Seventh, even after gaining authority, he did not pursue personal enrichment or consolidate wealth and power for individual benefit. Eighth, the broader legal and ethical framework that developed was not structured around empowering a specific economic class over another.

It is also important to note that this was not framed as a movement specifically centered on the abolition of slavery in the modern sense. Forms of servitude continued to exist within the historical context, and being Muslim did not necessarily mean that an individual was no longer enslaved, which further challenges the attempt to interpret it as a class based liberation movement.

For these reasons, the application of this theoretical model appears misplaced and unsupported by the historical record. It would be more appropriate to engage with the historical sources of Islam directly before placing it into external frameworks in a casual manner, as doing so risks reinforcing oversimplified and inaccurate assumptions.

> **@artalbomru** | 👍 2 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MX93U4KzA28&lc=UgxFN6dert9KAq8ArAR4AaABAg.ATwJML1iN8JAUfHEIUwGT-)  
>
> By 2026, Walter Mischel’s famous “marshmallow test” cannot be considered completely debunked, but its interpretation has become far more nuanced and complex. The core criticism that emerged around 2018 is now widely accepted: the key factor behind success turned out not to be self-control itself, but the socio-economic context in which a child grows up.  
>   
> Here’s a brief analysis of the test’s relevance today:  
>   
> · The key critique (2018). Tyler Watts’ study of 900 children showed that the link between the ability to wait and future success disappears once family income and stability are taken into account. Children from affluent families waited longer because they trusted adult promises (treating it like a “bank deposit”), while for children from disadvantaged backgrounds, taking the immediate reward was a more rational choice.  
> · Modern refinements (2020). Research has moved beyond individual traits. A 2020 experiment showed that children are willing to wait significantly longer when their decision affects a friend’s outcome (“interdependence”). This demonstrates that self-control is heavily influenced by social connections and collective goals.  
> · The author’s view. Mischel himself never claimed the test “doomed” children. He emphasized that the ability to delay gratification is a skill (the “cool system”) that can be trained by strengthening the prefrontal cortex and learning to think about consequences.  
>   
> Thus, in 2026, the marshmallow test is relevant not as a predictive tool for a child’s destiny, but as a model for understanding how environment, trust in the world, and social relationships shape our behavior.
